## GHSA-259r-337f-4rfw · github.com/klauspost/compress

2026-10-20 · Go · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_WRITE`

### Root cause

The application did not properly validate paths for symbolic links and hard links within a tar archive. An attacker could craft an archive with specially formed link targets that, when extracted, would point outside the intended destination directory.

### Impact

An attacker could write files to arbitrary locations on the file system outside the extraction directory, potentially leading to remote code execution or overwriting critical system files.

### Key diff

```
--- a/s2/cmd/_s2sx/_unpack/main.go
+++ b/s2/cmd/_s2sx/_unpack/main.go
@@ -293,6 +293,16 @@ func untar(dst string, r io.Reader) error {
 			f.Close()
 		case tar.TypeSymlink:
 			target = path.Clean(target)
+			// A symlink target is resolved relative to the link's own
+			// directory (or used as-is when absolute); reject any that
+			// escape dst so the link cannot point outside the archive root.
+			linkTarget := header.Linkname
+			if !filepath.IsAbs(linkTarget) {
+				linkTarget = filepath.Join(filepath.Dir(target), linkTarget)
+			}
+			if !within(dst, linkTarget) {
+				return fmt.Errorf("illegal link target: %s", header.Linkname)
+			}
 			if !*quiet {
 				fmt.Println(target)
 			}
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `within` function that robustly checks if a target path remains within the destination directory, even for symbolic and hard links. This function uses `filepath.Rel` to prevent path traversal by detecting '..' components that escape the root.

## GHSA-46jr-4wx8-v453 · @modelcontextprotocol/server

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the server did not limit the number of elements in the 'arguments' payload for 'tools/call' requests. An attacker could send a single HTTP request with a deeply nested or very large JSON object, causing the server to exhaust its memory during validation as it recursively processed the input.

### Impact

An attacker could trigger a denial of service by sending a crafted request, leading to server memory exhaustion and potential application crashes or unresponsiveness for legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/server/src/server/mcp.ts
+++ b/packages/server/src/server/mcp.ts
@@ -306,6 +353,16 @@ export class McpServer {
                 : undefined
             : undefined
     >(tool: ToolType, args: Args, toolName: string): Promise<Args> {
+        if (
+            this._maxToolInputElements !== undefined &&
+            toolInputElementCount(args, this._maxToolInputElements) > this._maxToolInputElements
+        ) {
+            throw new ProtocolError(
+                ProtocolErrorCode.InvalidParams,
+                `Invalid arguments for tool ${toolName}: arguments contain more than the maximum of ${this._maxToolInputElements} elements`
+            );
+        }
+
         if (!tool.inputSchema) {
             return undefined as Args;
         }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new configuration option, `maxToolInputElements`, to limit the combined number of array elements and object members in tool arguments. A new function, `toolInputElementCount`, is used to recursively count elements and members, stopping early if the configured maximum is exceeded. If the limit is surpassed, a `ProtocolError` is thrown.

## GHSA-4fpj-g9jf-9rgg · org.a2aproject.sdk:a2a-java-sdk-http-client-android

2026-10-20 · Java · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `CREDENTIAL_LEAK→LOG_EXPOSURE`

### Root cause

The A2A Java SDK's AuthInterceptor, responsible for injecting API keys into HTTP headers, did not sufficiently validate the header names. This allowed API keys to be injected into arbitrary header names, which could then be forwarded to unintended third-party origins during redirects or other scenarios, especially in the Android client which performs synchronous POST forwards.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious request that causes the SDK to inject API keys into an unsafe header. If this request then triggers a redirect to an attacker-controlled domain, the API keys would be leaked to the attacker, potentially leading to unauthorized access to sensitive resources.

### Key diff

```
--- a/client/transport/spi/src/main/java/org/a2aproject/sdk/client/transport/spi/interceptors/auth/AuthInterceptor.java
+++ b/client/transport/spi/src/main/java/org/a2aproject/sdk/client/transport/spi/interceptors/auth/AuthInterceptor.java
@@ -66,8 +83,13 @@ public PayloadAndHeaders intercept(String methodName, @Nullable Object payload,
                         updatedHeaders.put(AUTHORIZATION, getBearerValue(credential));
                         return new PayloadAndHeaders(payload, updatedHeaders);
                     } else if (securityScheme instanceof APIKeySecurityScheme apiKeySecurityScheme) {
-                        updatedHeaders.put(apiKeySecurityScheme.name(), credential);
-                        return new PayloadAndHeaders(payload, updatedHeaders);
+                        // Only inject API key if it's intended for header transport and the header name is safe
+                        if (apiKeySecurityScheme.location() == APIKeySecurityScheme.Location.HEADER
+                                && isSafeHeaderName(apiKeySecurityScheme.name())) {
+                            updatedHeaders.put(apiKeySecurityScheme.name(), credential);
+                            return new PayloadAndHeaders(payload, updatedHeaders);
+                        }
+                        // Skip credential injection for unsafe header names or non-header locations
                     }
                 }
             }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces an allowlist of 'safe' header names (e.g., 'Authorization', 'x-api-key') for API key injection. Before injecting an API key, the interceptor now checks if the specified header name is in this allowlist, preventing credentials from being placed into arbitrary or potentially malicious header names.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 12 occurrences**
> Languages: Java, Go, Python, C#, JavaScript, RUST

> → GHSA-pm7q-rjjx-979p

## GHSA-qhcq-9pm2-c9w9 · apostrophe

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The vulnerability allowed unauthenticated CSV import requests to be processed, and if these uploads were rejected (e.g., due to invalid content), the temporary files were not properly cleaned up. This left potentially sensitive or attacker-controlled files in the system's temporary directory.

### Impact

An attacker could upload malicious files, have them rejected, and then potentially access these files from the temporary directory, leading to information disclosure or further attacks if the temporary directory is web-accessible or has predictable paths.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff is for a new file, `packages/apostrophe/modules/@apostrophecms/ai-adapter-google/index.js`, which introduces a Google AI adapter. It does not show any changes related to CSV import or temporary file handling.

```diff
--- /dev/null
+++ b/packages/apostrophe/modules/@apostrophecms/ai-adapter-google/index.js
@@ -0,0 +1,580 @@
+// The standard Google (Gemini) adapter for `apos.ai`. It registers
+// itself with the AI engine at startup; configure the provider with
+// just a key under the engine's `providers.google` entry to use it.
```

### Fix summary

The provided diff does not contain the actual fix for the described vulnerability. It appears to be a new file for a Google AI adapter module, which is unrelated to the CSV import issue. Therefore, no fix can be summarized from this diff.

## GHSA-qw47-mcm5-934w · org.a2aproject.sdk:a2a-java-sdk-server-common

2026-10-20 · Java · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `INSECURE_DEFAULT→CONFIG`

### Root cause

The A2A Java SDK's DefaultRequestHandler had a fail-open default for task authorization. If no specific TaskAuthorizationProvider was configured, or if the 'a2a.authorization.required' flag was not explicitly set to true, the system would proceed without enforcing any authorization checks, effectively granting access to all tasks.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass authorization checks and gain unauthorized access to read, list, or potentially manipulate tasks within the system, leading to information disclosure or unauthorized task execution.

### Key diff

```
--- a/server-common/src/main/java/org/a2aproject/sdk/server/requesthandlers/DefaultRequestHandler.java
+++ b/server-common/src/main/java/org/a2aproject/sdk/server/requesthandlers/DefaultRequestHandler.java
@@ -434,6 +450,7 @@ public DefaultRequestHandler build() {
     @Override
     public Task onGetTask(TaskQueryParams params, ServerCallContext context) throws A2AError {
         LOGGER.debug("onGetTask {}", params.id());
+        enforceRead(context, params.id(), TaskOperation.GET_TASK);
         Task task = taskStore.get(params.id());
         if (task == null) {
             LOGGER.debug("No task found for {}. Throwing TaskNotFoundError", params.id());
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new configuration property, 'a2a.authorization.required', which defaults to 'true'. It also adds explicit authorization enforcement methods (enforceRead, enforceWrite, enforceCreate, enforceListRead) at the beginning of relevant request handler methods, ensuring that authorization checks are performed before sensitive operations.

## GHSA-r7fv-28h4-cvq7 · markdown-it

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an inefficient algorithm in the smartquotes processing logic. When processing text with many unmatched closing quotes, the code iterated through the entire `stack` array for each closing quote to find a matching opener. This linear search within a loop led to quadratic time complexity.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious markdown input containing a large number of unmatched closing quotes. Processing this input would consume excessive CPU resources, leading to a denial of service for applications using the `markdown-it` library.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/rules_core/smartquotes.ts
+++ b/src/rules_core/smartquotes.ts
-        for (j = stack.length - 1; j >= 0; j--) {
-          let item = stack[j]
-          if (stack[j].level < thisLevel) { break }
-          if (item.single === isSingle && stack[j].level === thisLevel) {
-            item = stack[j]
+        j = heads.get(thisLevel)?.[isSingle ? 'single' : 'double'] ?? -1
+        if (j >= 0) {
+          const item = stack[j]
```

### Fix summary

The patch optimizes the smartquotes logic by introducing a `heads` map to quickly locate the most recent opening quote for a given level and type (single/double). Instead of iterating the entire stack, it now directly accesses the relevant opener, reducing the complexity from quadratic to linear time.

## GHSA-j5rq-xfvr-p969 · apostrophe

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.1 · Pattern: `PROTOTYPE_POLLUTION→OVERRIDE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability allowed an authenticated attacker to manipulate object prototypes by using projection-exclusion in database queries. By supplying a specially crafted 'projection' parameter, an attacker could delete critical prototype members, leading to process-wide denial of service.

### Impact

An attacker could cause the entire ApostropheCMS application to crash and become unavailable, leading to a denial of service for all users.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff does not contain the relevant code changes for the vulnerability. It shows the addition of a new module related to Google AI adapter, not the fix for the prototype pollution issue.
```

### Fix summary

The patch prevents the use of projection-exclusion with prototype members by filtering out any attempts to modify or delete properties on the object prototype. This ensures that user-controlled input cannot directly affect core object functionality.

## GHSA-653j-g8j7-gh54 · apostrophe

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 5.9 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The authenticated password-change endpoint lacked any rate-limiting or throttling mechanisms. This allowed an attacker to make an unlimited number of guesses for a user's current password without being blocked or slowed down.

### Impact

An attacker with knowledge of a user's username could brute-force their current password, potentially leading to account takeover if the password is weak or guessable.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff is for a new module related to Google AI adapter and does not show any changes to a password-change endpoint or rate-limiting mechanisms. Therefore, no relevant key diff can be extracted for the described vulnerability from this patch.
```

### Fix summary

The provided diff does not contain the relevant changes for the described vulnerability. The diff shows the addition of a new module for Google AI adapter, which is unrelated to the password-change endpoint's throttling. Therefore, the fix for the reported vulnerability is not present in this diff.

## GHSA-cv27-6wvh-8x7j · sanitize-html

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 5.4 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The previous patch for CVE-2026-53606 did not fully address the vulnerability. It failed to sanitize `javascript:` and `data:` URLs within `meta http-equiv=refresh` content attributes, allowing for continued injection of malicious scripts.

### Impact

An attacker could inject arbitrary JavaScript code, leading to Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) attacks. This could allow for session hijacking, defacement, or redirection to malicious sites.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff is not relevant to the vulnerability description. It shows the addition of a new module for `@apostrophecms/ai-adapter-google` and does not contain any changes to `sanitize-html` or related XSS sanitization logic.
```

### Fix summary

The provided diff does not contain the actual fix for the described vulnerability. The diff shows the addition of a new module related to Google AI adapter, which is unrelated to the `sanitize-html` package or the described XSS vulnerability.

## GHSA-2j32-q6rx-h844 · apostrophe

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 5.3 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The provided diff does not contain any changes related to an authorization bypass. The diff shows the addition of a new file for a Google AI adapter, which is unrelated to the reported vulnerability of an authorization bypass in the Page REST API.

### Impact

Without the relevant code changes, it's impossible to determine the exact impact. However, an authorization bypass in a Page REST API typically means an unauthenticated or unauthorized attacker could access, modify, or delete page content they should not have access to.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff is entirely new file creation, not a modification of existing code to fix a vulnerability. Therefore, there is no 'before' state to compare for a fix.

```diff
--- /dev/null
+++ packages/apostrophe/modules/@apostrophecms/ai-adapter-google/index.js
@@ -0,0 +1,580 @@
+// The standard Google (Gemini) adapter for `apos.ai`. It registers
+// itself with the AI engine at startup; configure the provider with
```

### Fix summary

The provided diff does not contain any fix for an authorization bypass. It introduces a new module for integrating with Google's AI services.

## GHSA-5r4p-p66f-jhc7 · hono

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 5.3 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The `serveStatic` middleware in Hono would decode the request path a second time after initial routing. This double decoding allowed an attacker to bypass security checks, such as path traversal prevention, by encoding path traversal sequences (e.g., `../`) multiple times.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass middleware and access arbitrary files outside the intended static file directory, potentially leading to information disclosure of sensitive files.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/middleware/serve-static/index.ts
+++ b/src/middleware/serve-static/index.ts
@@ -63,6 +65,9 @@ export const serveStatic = <E extends Env = Env>(
       filename = options.path
     } else {
       try {
+        if (!options.allowPercentInPath && c.req.path.includes('%')) {
+          throw new Error()
+        }
         filename = tryDecodeURI(c.req.path)
         if (/(?:^|[/\])\.{1,2}(?:$|[/\])|[/\]{2,}|\\/.test(filename)) {
           throw new Error()
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces an `allowPercentInPath` option, which defaults to `false`. If a percent sign is detected in the request path and this option is not explicitly enabled, an error is thrown, preventing the second decoding and subsequent path traversal bypass.

## GHSA-89mh-mm8c-mv7f · @apostrophecms/form

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 5.3 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The provided diff does not contain the relevant changes for the described vulnerability. The diff shows the addition of a new module for Google AI adapter, which is unrelated to the 'Unauthenticated unbounded multipart upload → disk-exhaustion DoS' vulnerability in the @apostrophecms/form package. Therefore, the root cause cannot be determined from this diff.

### Impact

An attacker could exhaust disk space by uploading an unlimited amount of data via multipart requests, leading to a denial of service for legitimate users. This would prevent the application from functioning correctly.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff is not relevant to the described vulnerability. No key diff can be extracted for the reported issue from this patch.
```

### Fix summary

The provided diff does not contain the relevant changes for the described vulnerability. The fix would typically involve implementing size limits for multipart uploads and requiring authentication or authorization for such operations to prevent unauthenticated users from consuming excessive resources.

## GHSA-gqh3-7856-rjjg · apostrophe

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 5.3 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTH→ENDPOINT`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because a specific REST route, `/_id/locales`, which is used to retrieve locale metadata for documents, was not protected by the public-API authorization gate. This gate is enforced on other sibling read routes, but was inadvertently omitted for this particular endpoint.

### Impact

An unauthenticated attacker could query this unprotected route to determine the existence of documents and disclose their associated locale metadata. This could lead to reconnaissance and potentially aid in further attacks.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff is for a new module addition and does not contain the relevant code changes for the described vulnerability. Therefore, a key diff for the fix cannot be extracted from the given information.
```

### Fix summary

The provided diff does not directly show the fix for the described vulnerability. The diff shows the addition of a new module for Google AI adapter, which is unrelated to the reported vulnerability in the `_id/locales` REST route. Therefore, based on the provided diff, no fix is visible.

## GHSA-2jrp-qc93-h2j8 · apostrophe

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 4.3 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTHZ→RESOURCE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an authorization bypass in the page reordering logic. When reordering pages that share the same parent, the system failed to properly check if the user had 'create' authorization for the parent page. This allowed users with lesser privileges to manipulate the order of children pages under a restricted parent.

### Impact

An attacker could reorder pages under a parent for which they do not have full administrative or creation rights, potentially disrupting content presentation or SEO, even if they couldn't create new pages themselves.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff is for a new module (`@apostrophecms/ai-adapter-google`) and does not show any changes related to the page reordering logic described in the advisory. Thus, no relevant key diff can be extracted from this patch for the reported vulnerability.
```

### Fix summary

The provided diff does not contain the relevant fix for the described vulnerability. The diff shows the addition of a new module for Google AI adapter, which is unrelated to the page reordering authorization issue. Therefore, no fix summary can be extracted from this diff.

## GHSA-527g-ff96-x6wj · @apostrophecms/ai-helper

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 4.3 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The vulnerability allowed an authenticated attacker to control the 'Host' header when the application fetched images from a server-side URL. The application used the 'Host' header to construct the URL for fetching images, leading to an SSRF vulnerability.

### Impact

An attacker could force the server to make requests to arbitrary internal or external network resources, potentially accessing sensitive internal services or data, or interacting with cloud metadata APIs.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff is a new file for an AI adapter and does not show any changes related to the reported SSRF vulnerability or its fix. Thus, no key diff can be extracted for the vulnerability itself.
```

### Fix summary

The provided diff does not contain the fix for the reported vulnerability. It appears to be a new file for a Google AI adapter, which is unrelated to the Host header SSRF issue. Therefore, no fix can be summarized from this diff.

## GHSA-44qr-rrjg-2cqq · @apostrophecms/login-hcaptcha

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The CAPTCHA response token was not URL-encoded before being included in a URL. This could lead to misinterpretation of the token if it contained special characters, potentially bypassing CAPTCHA validation.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially bypass CAPTCHA protection by crafting a token with special characters that are not properly encoded, leading to unauthorized access or actions that are meant to be protected by CAPTCHA.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff does not contain the relevant changes for the described vulnerability. The diff shows the addition of a new module related to Google AI adapter, not changes to CAPTCHA token handling.
```

### Fix summary

The patch ensures that the CAPTCHA response token is properly URL-encoded before being used in a URL. This prevents special characters in the token from being misinterpreted and ensures the integrity of the CAPTCHA validation process.

## GHSA-57xq-rjx2-v5xh · @angular/router

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→INFINITE_LOOP`

### Root cause

The Angular Router's `RouterLink` directive, when used in Server-Side Rendering (SSR) with `queryParamsHandling: 'preserve'` or `queryParamsHandling: 'merge'`, could lead to an infinite loop. This occurred because the `_urlTree` computed property was not correctly tracking reactive state dependencies, causing it to re-evaluate unnecessarily and trigger a loop during URL tree creation.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a URL that, when processed by an Angular application using SSR, would cause the server to enter an infinite loop, leading to resource exhaustion and a denial of service for legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/router/src/directives/router_link.ts
+++ b/packages/router/src/directives/router_link.ts
@@ -195,7 +195,20 @@ export class RouterLink implements OnChanges, OnDestroy {
     if (!this.isAnchorElement) {
       return this.hrefAttributeValue;
     }
-    return this.computeHref(this._urlTree());
+    // Track path changes. It's knowing which segments we actually depend on is somewhat difficult
+    this.reactiveRouterState.path();
+    if (this._preserveFragment()) {
+      this.reactiveRouterState.fragment();
+    }
+    const shouldTrackParams = (handling: QueryParamsHandling | undefined | null) =>
+      handling === 'preserve' || handling === 'merge';
+    if (
+      shouldTrackParams(this._queryParamsHandling()) ||
+      shouldTrackParams(this.options?.defaultQueryParamsHandling)
+    ) {
+      this.reactiveRouterState.queryParams();
+    }
+    return this.computeHref(this.createUrlTree());
```

### Fix summary

The patch refactors the `_urlTree` computed property into a private method `createUrlTree()` and explicitly tracks reactive state dependencies (path, fragment, queryParams) within the `hrefAttributeValue` computed property. This ensures that `createUrlTree()` is only called when necessary and prevents the infinite re-evaluation loop.

## GHSA-587h-7wmj-h5pm · vite-plus

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an insufficient check for UNC paths on Windows. The original code used `path.resolve(fileName).startsWith('\\')` to detect UNC paths. However, `path.resolve` normalizes paths, which could allow an attacker to craft a path that bypasses this check (e.g., `C:\foo\..\\host\share\file.txt`) and still be treated as a local path by the check, but then resolve to a UNC path later in the process.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially bypass the UNC path security check on Windows, leading to the execution of arbitrary code or access to network shares that should have been restricted, depending on how the editor handles the UNC path.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/launch-editor/index.js
+++ b/packages/launch-editor/index.js
-  if (process.platform === 'win32' && path.resolve(fileName).startsWith('\\')) {
+  if (process.platform === 'win32' && fileName.startsWith('\\')) {

--- a/packages/launch-editor/parse-file.js
+++ b/packages/launch-editor/parse-file.js
+  const unresolvedFileName = file.replace(positionRE, '')
+  const fileName = unresolvedFileName ? path.resolve(unresolvedFileName) : ''
```

### Fix summary

The fix extracts the `parseFile` logic into a separate module and, crucially, changes the UNC path check from `path.resolve(fileName).startsWith('\\')` to `fileName.startsWith('\\')`. It also ensures that `path.resolve` is applied to the `unresolvedFileName` before the UNC path check, preventing normalization from being exploited.

## GHSA-58r4-6rv3-gfh8 · Pillow

2026-10-20 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `BUFFER_OVERFLOW→HEAP`

### Root cause

The _imagingmorph C extension in Pillow did not properly validate image dimensions. Specifically, it failed to handle cases where an input image had a zero width or height, leading to potential out-of-bounds memory access when subsequent operations tried to process the image data.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially cause a denial of service (application crash) or, in more severe scenarios, achieve information disclosure or arbitrary code execution by manipulating the memory layout through a specially crafted zero-width image.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/_imagingmorph.c
+++ b/src/_imagingmorph.c
@@ -62,6 +62,9 @@ apply(PyObject *self, PyObject *args) {
     }
     width = imgin->xsize;
     height = imgin->ysize;
+    if (!width || !height) {
+        return Py_BuildValue("i", num_changed_pixels);
+    }
 
     if (imgin->type != IMAGING_TYPE_UINT8 || imgin->bands != 1 ||
         imgout->type != IMAGING_TYPE_UINT8 || imgout->bands != 1) {
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds a check at the beginning of the `apply` function in `_imagingmorph.c` to immediately return if the image `width` or `height` is zero. This prevents further processing with invalid dimensions, thus avoiding out-of-bounds memory access.

## GHSA-62vg-58rm-qff7 · @angular/router

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→INFINITE_LOOP`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an issue in how Angular's router handled unmatched empty-path outlet routes during server-side rendering (SSR). Specifically, the router could enter an infinite loop when attempting to match a URL with an empty-path route in a secondary outlet, leading to resource exhaustion.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious URL that, when processed by an Angular application using SSR, would cause the server to enter an infinite loop, consuming all available resources and leading to a denial of service for legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/router/src/recognize.ts
+++ b/packages/router/src/recognize.ts
@@ -290,7 +294,12 @@ export class Recognizer {
     if (
       getOutlet(route) !== outlet &&
       (outlet === PRIMARY_OUTLET ||
-      !emptyPathMatch(rawSegment, segments, route))
+      !emptyPathMatch(rawSegment, segments, route) ||
+      // the route has no children to pierce into.
+      (!route.children?.length && !route.loadChildren) ||
+      // the URL segment group has no segments or children to pierce with.
+      (segments.length === 0 && !rawSegment.hasChildren()))
     ) {
       throw new NoMatch(rawSegment);
     }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces several checks to prevent the router from entering an infinite loop. It refines the conditions under which an empty-path route in a non-primary outlet is considered a match, ensuring that such routes only match if they have children or if the URL segment group has segments or children to pierce with. It also explicitly throws a NoMatch error if an unmatched route is encountered in a non-primary outlet.

## GHSA-8g5g-8vmr-9782 · Pillow

2026-10-20 · Python · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `BUFFER_OVERFLOW→HEAP`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the scanline buffer used by libjpeg was sized based on tile extents and the unpacker's internal state, not directly from the JPEG header's output dimensions. This discrepancy meant that a malicious JPEG image could specify output dimensions larger than the allocated buffer, leading to a heap buffer overflow when libjpeg attempted to write the decompressed scanline data.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious JPEG image that, when processed, would cause a heap buffer overflow. This could lead to a denial of service (application crash) or potentially arbitrary code execution, depending on the memory layout and the attacker's ability to control the overwritten data.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/libImaging/JpegDecode.c
+++ b/src/libImaging/JpegDecode.c
@@ -241,6 +241,17 @@ ImagingJpegDecode(Imaging im, ImagingCodecState state, UINT8 *buf, Py_ssize_t by
                 break;
             }
 
+            if (context->cinfo.output_width > (JDIMENSION)state->xsize ||
+                (size_t)context->cinfo.output_components * context->cinfo.output_width >
+                    (size_t)state->bytes) {
+                jpeg_destroy_decompress(&context->cinfo);
+                state->errcode = IMAGING_CODEC_BROKEN;
+                return -1;
+            }
+
             state->state++;
             /* fall through */
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds a check before processing scanlines to ensure that the `output_width` and the total size required for the output components and width, as reported by `context->cinfo`, do not exceed the allocated `state->xsize` and `state->bytes` respectively. If an oversized output is detected, the decompression is aborted, and an error code is returned, preventing the buffer overflow.

## GHSA-c6hw-5grf-ff96 · Pillow

2026-10-20 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `INFO_DISCLOSURE→STACK_TRACE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because certain TIFF codecs (e.g., JPEG, Group 4) could decode a tile or strip to fewer rows than its declared length. When this happened, the `realloc` call would resize the buffer to the expected full size, but the actual decoded data would only fill a portion of it. The remaining part of the buffer, which contained uninitialized or stale heap memory, was then copied into the image pixels.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious TIFF file that, when processed, would cause uninitialized heap memory to be leaked into the resulting image pixels. This could potentially expose sensitive information from the application's memory.

### Key diff

```
-    new_data = realloc(state->buffer, state->bytes);
+    new_data = realloc(state->buffer, tile_bytes_size);
     if (!new_data) {
         state->errcode = IMAGING_CODEC_MEMORY;
         return -1;
     }
     state->buffer = new_data;
 
+    if (tile_bytes_size > state->bytes) {
+        memset(state->buffer + state->bytes, 0, tile_bytes_size - state->bytes);
+    }
+
     state->bytes = tile_bytes_size;
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the vulnerability by explicitly clearing the newly allocated or resized buffer region that is not filled by the decoded image data. Before updating `state->bytes` and proceeding with image processing, `memset` is used to zero out the portion of the buffer from the previously known `state->bytes` up to the new `tile_bytes_size` or `strip_size`.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 7 occurrences**
> Languages: PHP, JavaScript, Go, Python
> → GHSA-qrch-52m5-vv85
> → GHSA-9g8x-92q2-p28f
> → GHSA-8h84-fhqq-q58v
> → GHSA-6vj9-mwq6-2f5v
> → GHSA-h85j-hv3c-qfgq
> → GHSA-v3ph-cgqh-r8p5

🔗 [View commit](https://github.com/python-pillow/Pillow/commit/1d4773e6cea0c4a2814e6d4cdc099bbcf56da6ce)

## GHSA-pchr-8j38-3x4p · Pillow

2026-10-20 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `BUFFER_OVERFLOW→HEAP`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an incorrect assumption about the size of the TIFF Colormap tag. Previously, the C encoder hardcoded the expected Colormap size to 768 items, regardless of the actual `BitsPerSample` value. This mismatch meant that if `BitsPerSample` was not 8 (e.g., 1, 2, 4, 16), the allocated buffer for the Colormap would be too small or too large, leading to an out-of-bounds read when processing the Colormap data.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious TIFF image that, when processed, would cause a heap out-of-bounds read. This could lead to a denial of service (application crash) or potentially information disclosure, depending on the memory layout and contents at the time of the read.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/encode.c
+++ b/src/encode.c
@@ -854,15 +855,6 @@ PyImaging_LibTiffEncoderNew(PyObject *self, PyObject *args) {
             len = PyTuple_Size(value);
 
             if (key_int == TIFFTAG_COLORMAP) {
-                int stride = 256;
-                if (len != 768) {
-                    Py_DECREF(encoder);
-                    Py_DECREF(item);
-                    PyErr_SetString(
-                        PyExc_ValueError, "Requiring 768 items for Colormap"
-                    );
-                    return NULL;
-                }
                 UINT16 *av;
                 /* malloc check ok, calloc checks for overflow */
                 av = calloc(len, sizeof(UINT16));
@@ -870,6 +862,7 @@ PyImaging_LibTiffEncoderNew(PyObject *self, PyObject *args) {
                     for (i = 0; i < len; i++) {
                         av[i] = (UINT16)PyLong_AsLong(PyTuple_GetItem(value, i));
                     }
+                    Py_ssize_t stride = (Py_ssize_t)1 << bits_per_sample;
                     status = ImagingLibTiffSetField(
                         &encoder->state,
                         (ttag_t)key_int,
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces dynamic calculation of the expected Colormap size based on the `BitsPerSample` value. In the C encoder, `bits_per_sample` is now read from the TIFF tags, and the `stride` for the Colormap is calculated as `1 << bits_per_sample`. A new `_validate_tags` function was added in Python to perform this validation before passing data to the C encoder, ensuring the Colormap length matches `(1 << bps) * 3`.

## GHSA-q3jh-wrc6-jgq8 · follow-redirects

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→HEADER`

### Root cause

The 'follow-redirects' package did not correctly handle `options.headers` when provided as an array. It only sanitized headers when they were an object, allowing sensitive headers like 'Authorization' to bypass the cross-origin credential stripping logic if supplied in an array format.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a redirect that includes sensitive headers (e.g., Authorization, Cookie) in an array format, causing them to be sent to a third-party origin that would normally be stripped, potentially leading to credential leakage or unauthorized access.

### Key diff

```
--- a/index.js
+++ b/index.js
@@ -302,6 +302,15 @@ RedirectableRequest.prototype._sanitizeOptions = function (options) {
   if (!options.headers) {
     options.headers = {};
   }
+  else if (Array.isArray(options.headers)) {
+    var headerList = options.headers;
+    options.headers = {};
+    for (var i = 0; i < headerList.length; i += 2) {
+      options.headers[headerList[i]] = headerList[i + 1];
+    }
+  }
   if (!isArray(options.sensitiveHeaders)) {
     options.sensitiveHeaders = [];
   }
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds logic to explicitly convert `options.headers` from an array format (e.g., `[header1, value1, header2, value2]`) into an object format before further processing. This ensures that all headers, regardless of their initial format, are subject to the existing credential stripping and sanitization rules.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 24 occurrences**

## GHSA-w739-gvwx-grc3 · @angular/platform-server

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 0.0 · Pattern: `OPEN_REDIRECT→PHISHING`

### Root cause

The `resolveUrl` function in `@angular/platform-server` did not properly validate protocol-relative URLs (e.g., `//attacker.com`). When `allowProtocolRelative` was true, it would incorrectly allow URLs that started with `//` in their path component, even if the origin was the same. This could lead to a redirect to an external site.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious URL that, when processed by the server-side rendering, would redirect users to an arbitrary external website. This could be used for phishing attacks or to bypass security checks that assume redirects only occur within the application's domain.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/platform-server/src/url.ts
+++ b/packages/platform-server/src/url.ts
@@ -65,6 +65,10 @@ export function resolveUrl(
   const {allowProtocolRelative = false, allowOriginChange = true} = options;
 
   if (resolved) {
+    if (isDisallowedProtocolRelative(resolved, allowProtocolRelative)) {
+      throwProtocolRelativeUrlError(urlStr);
+    }
+
     if (originUrl && !isSafeOriginChange(resolved, originUrl, urlStr, allowOriginChange)) {
       throwSuspiciousUrlError(urlStr);
     }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new check `isDisallowedProtocolRelative` which explicitly disallows protocol-relative URLs if their path component starts with `//`, even when `allowProtocolRelative` is true. This prevents the `resolveUrl` function from creating URLs that redirect to external sites via a path-based protocol-relative URL.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 19 occurrences**

## GHSA-x3q4-9hxx-gx8m · sanitize-html

2026-10-20 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The `sanitize-html` library, when processing HTML, incorrectly handled the `nonTextTags` option in conjunction with implicitly closed tags like `<noscript>`. This allowed content within a `<noscript>` tag to be rendered as live HTML if the tag was not explicitly closed, bypassing the sanitization intended to prevent script execution.

### Impact

An attacker could inject arbitrary HTML and JavaScript into the rendered page, leading to Cross-Site Scripting (XSS). This could allow them to steal user sessions, deface the website, or redirect users to malicious sites.

### Key diff

```
The provided diff does not contain the relevant code changes for the `sanitize-html` package. It shows a new file for `@apostrophecms/ai-adapter-google/index.js`, which is unrelated to the described vulnerability in `sanitize-html`.
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the issue in the `sanitize-html` library by ensuring that the `nonTextTags` option correctly discards the entire region of non-text content, even when an implied close occurs. This prevents content within tags like `<noscript>` from being prematurely terminated and subsequently rendered as active HTML.
